Letter from the Director
Last fall the Montana Fish and Wildlife Commission set the Montana hunting regulations for 2026 and 2027.
In doing so, it made a variety of changes to address concerns about nonresident hunting pressure and mule
deer numbers.
Though most of the regulations stayed the same from last year, the changes adopted by the commission
garnered the most interest. And that’s expected. Just like FWP staf, the commission has heard clearly
that hunters and landowners are concerned with declining mule deer numbers and hunting pressure from
nonresident hunters.
So, what has changed? Before I point to a few changes in this year’s regulations, it’s important to remember
what the commission, the Montana Legislature, and FWP have done in the past few years to try and protect
declining mule deer herds.
Starting in 2023 FWP began reducing deer B licenses in areas with declining mule deer numbers. That same
year the legislature also passed a law that said nonresident hunters could only have one deer B license
unless they had a combination license, then they could have two. The following year, FWP continued to
reduce deer B licenses in many areas to the lowest number allowed by the commission established quota.
This fall the commission reduced the number of deer licenses a resident can hold from eight to three. The
commission also made deer B licenses only valid on private land within most of the state.
To address hunting pressure, the commission passed a cap on the sale of a particular nonresident deer license. Based on recent drawing
statistics, this cap would essentially cut the number of these licenses available to nonresidents by about 2,500.
In proposing the change, Commission Chair Lesley Robinson pointed to the increase in hunting pressure she and other landowners are
seeing and the need to try something diferent.
So as a hunter in Montana, what should you take away from all these changes? I would ofer a couple of points.
First, FWP and the commission continue to work hard at protecting Montana’s mule deer herds. This happens not only through regulations,
but also with sound science and public engagement. Before the next biennial season setting process, FWP will fnalize a new mule deer
management plan that will be greatly informed by public feedback.
Second, your input and involvement are critical. While we saw hundreds of comments through our regulations setting process, we also
held some meetings that were poorly attended. We have ample opportunity for the public to be involved. If you’re interested in helping
to shape future regulations, sign up for emails, call your regional ofce, make sure you’re getting our newsletters so you know what’s
going on.
Lastly, hunters can have an impact on landowners and the resources. We know the majority of hunters behave ethically and responsibly,
but those who don’t, even though they are in the extreme minority, are having a big impact on landowners.
If you’re out hunting this year and see unethical or illegal behavior, please report it. You can fnd your local game warden easily by going
to tipmont.mt.gov. Also, take the time to refresh yourself on hunting ethics and take the Hunter Landowner Stewardship Program. You
can fnd it online at fwp.mt.gov/itsuptous.
Good luck this year and be safe.
Christy Clark

Highlights
• Resident deer hunters are limited to purchasing three deer licenses per license year. That may be a general license and
two B licenses or three B licenses.
• Many mule deer B licenses are now restricted to private land only. Be sure to check the opportunity specifc details for
each hunt.
• Over-the-counter Deer B and Elk B License purchase date changed to June 15.
• Hunting and fshing licenses are accessible through a secure and convenient mobile app. The Montana MyFWP app
provides a digital wallet to store and display licenses and digital carcass tags, known as E-Tags, that can validate your
harvest in the feld.
• Photocopying or printing more than one license may constitute a violation. It is unlawful to apply for, purchase, or possess
more licenses, permits, or tags than are authorized. See page 25.
• Permits for either-sex elk or brow-tined bull-antlerless elk will limit the holder to hunting for antlered elk within only the
designated hunting district (HD) for the period identifed on the permit. Antlerless elk hunting will not be limited by the
permit. A general license may be used to hunt in any open district during dates outside of that identifed on the permit.
• Deadline for deer/elk permits and Nonresident Combination License applications is April 1.
• Nonresidents hunting with a Montana-licensed outftter applying for combination licenses may elect to purchase an
additional outftter preference point at the time of application.
• To prevent the spread of CWD, follow carcass disposal guidelines, see page 4.
Reminders
• Youth and Apprentice Hunter Information, see page 19.
• Game Damage Hunt Roster Rules, see page 31.
• License Discount Opportunities, see page 18.
• Elk hunters can purchase multiple Elk B licenses, see page 16.
• Landowners or their designees apply online or at an FWP ofce then submit a separate landowner preference form.
Preference forms for deer and elk permits must be received by April 1, and forms for Elk B, Deer B, Antelope and Antelope
B must be received by June 1.
• An email address is required of all applicants applying for limited licenses or permits.
How To Use These Regulations
Montana’s vast 147,164 square miles is divided into seven administrative
regions, each with a main regional ofce. Each region is then broken down
into hunting districts. The regions are numbered 1 through 7 with the hunting
districts in the region corresponding to that number. Example: Region 1
hunting districts start with 1 or 001, Region 2 starts with 2 or 002 etc.

Regulations are adopted by the Fish & Wildlife Commission
Regulations for season dates, structures, and restrictions were adopted by the F&W Commission on Dec. 4, 2025, under
the authority granted in MCA 87-1-301 and are valid March 1, 2026, through Feb. 28, 2027. The F&W Commission reserves
the authority to amend the seasons, limits and regulations herein if deemed necessary for wildlife management purposes.
Statutes and other information, including other agencies’ restrictions, are provided as a courtesy and do not fall under
commission authority. Christy Clark, Director.
Discrimination Prohibited – State and Federal laws, Title VI of Civil Rights Act of 1964, Section 504 of the Rehabilitation
Act of 1973, Title II of the Americans with Disabilities Act of 1990, the Age Discrimination Act of 1975, and Title IX of the
Education Amendments of 1972, prohibit discrimination on the basis of race, color, sex, age, religion, national origin, or
disability. Anyone believing he or she has been discriminated against (as described above) in any FWP program, activity,
or facility may write to FWP Human Resources, 1420 East Sixth Avenue, PO Box 200701, Helena, MT 59620-0701 or
Ofce of Diversity, Inclusion and Civil Rights, U.S. Department of the Interior, 1849 C Street, NW Washington, DC 20260.
Any changes made to this printed publication can be found on the FWP website or by inquiring at any local FWP ofce.

Archery Only Season
Donation Options MONTANA FWP
When purchasing your fshing, hunting, and recreation licenses you have the option to donate to diferent programs.
Below is a description of each.
Wolf Mitigation
Donations will be used by the Montana Department of Livestock to contract for wolf management with the U.S.
Department of Agriculture’s Wildlife Services for fight time, collaring, and lethal control of wolves.
Warm Water Fisheries Enhancement
This program supports projects that improve Montana’s warm water fsheries and habitats.
Hunters Against Hunger
Donations to this program help cover the cost of processing and distributing the meat that hunters donate to
feed hungry Montanans. There will be no cost or processing fees to hunters who drop meat of at participating
processors. For a list of processors visit mfbn.org/hunters-against-hunger/.
MT-PLAN
This DNRC grant program facilitates collaboration among recreationists, private landowners, and land management
agencies to enhance public access throughout the state.
Bowhunters with Disabilities Program
Donations to this program help charitable and nonproft organizations cover the costs of producing modifed
archery equipment designed to accommodate disabled hunters.

Youth Hunter
Special 2-day Youth Deer Hunt: October 15 & 16, 2026
Youth hunters and apprentice hunters 10-15 may hunt during the Youth Statewide Two-day Deer Hunt. See chart.
Youth Deer & Elk Hunts
Some hunting districts ofer special opportunities for youth ages 10-15. See hunting district specifc regulations and page 124.
Youth Elk Permits and Elk B Licenses
Elk permits and Elk B Licenses only for youth 12 (by Jan 16, 2027) -15 years of age to hunt elk as listed in the specifc hunting district
regulations have the following requirements:
To qualify, the applicant must:
• be 12 (by Jan 16, 2027) -15 years of age at time of application,
• show proof of successfully completing hunter education.
Youth that are 15 years of age at the start of the general deer and elk season are eligible to hunt in the youth areas with a general license
even if they turn 16 years of age during the season.
Free license for terminally ill youth (MCA 87-2-805)
• Montana law allows terminally ill resident or nonresident youth under 18 years of age to receive a free one-time Deer and Elk combi-
nation license (without bear), or a deer, elk, or antelope license/permit as applicable.
• The youth must be accompanied by a licensed outftter or an adult who has a license to hunt in Montana.
• Minimum age and hunter education requirements may be waived for this license. For information call Licensing at 406-444-2950.
Youth Hunter Opportunity Chart
Age Opportunities and Special Requirements
Available To Resident & Nonresident
An apprentice hunter is a resident or nonresident certifed at an FWP ofce or online. An apprentice hunter can certify as early
as age 9 but cannot buy a license until age 10. This license allows the apprentice to hunt some species, while accompanied by a
10
mentor, without frst completing a hunter education course. An apprentice hunting certifcation is limited to two years before a
and
person must complete a hunter education course. The apprentice hunter may not apply for or purchase a bighorn sheep, black
over
bear, mountain lion, or wolf license or a hunting license or permit with a limited quota. Other restrictions apply. See our website
for details at: fwp.mt.gov/hunt/education/apprentice-hunter
A youth 11 years of age who will reach 12 years of age by Jan. 16, 2027, may (after completing hunter education):
• Apply for any special drawing – must be able to show proof of having completed an approved hunter education course at the
11
time of application,
• After Aug. 15, 2026, hunt any game species for which their license is valid during an open season.
In order to carry or use a frearm in public, a youth under 14 years of age must be accompanied by a person having charge or
under
custody of the child; or be under the supervision of a qualifed frearms safety instructor or an adult 18 years of age or older who has
14
been authorized by the youth’s parent or guardian, as per Montana law 45-8-344.
Youth Statewide Two-Day Deer Hunt –
• The F&W Commission is allowing two additional days to provide a hunting opportunity to encourage youth participation in
deer hunting.
• Lawfully licensed hunters 10 through 15 years of age may hunt deer during the Statewide Two-day Youth Only Deer Hunt Oct.
15-16.
10-15 – During these two days, youth hunters with a general deer, deer permit or deer B license may take those deer species and
sex otherwise available on their general deer, deer permit or deer B license the frst day of the general frearm season in the
specifc hunting district the youth is hunting.
• A adult at least 18 years of age must accompany the youth hunter in the feld.
• Shooting hours, hunter education requirements, and all other regulations that apply to the general deer frearm season shall
apply to this youth two-day deer hunt.
Available To Nonresident Only
Nonresident Youth Combination Licenses will be sold to nonresident youth who have an adult immediate-family member
sponsor (applicant’s natural or adoptive parent, stepparent, grandparent, brother or sister who is 18 years of age or older
12-17
and possesses a current resident or nonresident deer or elk license). The youth must be accompanied by the sponsor while
hunting. Applications are available from FWP’s website and may be processed at any FWP ofce. For further details and an
application, contact Licensing at 406-444-2950.

Drawing Statistics & Bonus Points
Drawing Statistics
Following a drawing, FWP publishes the resulting drawing statistics online. Most drawings
conducted will have a corresponding drawing statistics report. These reports show the
number of applicants in each category (resident landowner, nonresident landowner, resident,
nonresident), the fnal quotas, number of applicants in each category, and the number of
successful applicants for each opportunity.
For a more detailed report, including how bonus points afected the success rates, FWP also
publishes a detailed bonus point drawing statistics report. These reports demonstrate the
proportion of successful applicants broken down by opportunity, residency, and the respective
amount of bonus points used in the drawing. Remember, the bonus point system is designed
to be completely random. This means the drawing statistics will vary with each drawing. For
instance, for one drawing the highest success rates might be with hunters who had four bonus Scan to access draw
points. While for another drawing the highest success rates might be for hunters who had statistics for all species
eight bonus points.
It’s important to note that the quotas printed in these regulations may vary from the fnal quotas drawn based on biological
surveys. For more information on FWP’s drawing statistics reports, please scan the QR code or visit the link below.
fwp.mt.gov/buyandapply/hunting-licenses/drawing-statistics
Bonus Points
In Montana, every hunter who applies for a license or permit gets one chance in the drawing. Bonus points let you add to
those chances. Think of bonus points as extra “rafe tickets”. If you apply for a license or permit without any bonus points,
you get one “rafe ticket.” The number of “rafe tickets” you have in the drawing goes up with the number of bonus points
you have.
A unique feature about Montana’s system is that FWP squares bonus points prior to drawing. So, the more bonus points
you have, the number of “rafe tickets” you have in the drawing goes up exponentially. For instance, if you have 10 bonus
points, you get 101 “rafe tickets” for the drawing – 100 for your squared bonus points and 1 for your current application:
(10 x 10) + 1.
HOW BONUS POINTS WORK
Formula: 1 Application + (# Bonus Points)2 = # Drawing Chances
Number of Bonus Points to Number of Drawing Chances

of

Nonresident Hunter Information
Montana has some amazing hunting opportunities for nonresident hunters. However, it’s
important to know a few key pieces of information before you apply.
Like many states, Montana law governs nonresident hunters diferently than resident
hunters.
• All nonresidents are limited to, but not guaranteed, 10% of the overall drawing quota for
an opportunity. That means if you apply for a deer permit in a hunting district with 100
permits, nonresidents can only draw 10 of those permits.
• Nonresident moose, sheep, and goat hunting districts with a quota less than 10 are added
together to a regional hunting district quota. For nonresidents, these opportunities
are calculated based of the entire quota for that region. The hunting districts where
nonresidents can apply rotate each year in situations like this.
Scan to watch how to apply
• The 10% cap includes both nonresident landowners and regular nonresidents; video
however, landowner preference is the frst stage of the drawing. Landowner preference
could account for up to 15% of the total quotas including residents and nonresidents.
• There is an exception to the 10% rule for nonresidents. If all residents who applied are successful in drawing,
nonresidents may be allocated more than 10% of the drawing quotas.
In Montana, nonresidents must be successful in the nonresident combo drawing to move on to the drawing for specifc
permits. The nonresident combo are the nonresident licenses you must hold to draw specifc deer or elk permits.
Nonresident combos are drawn based on preference points. Preference points are applied diferently than bonus points.
For the nonresident combo drawings, the applicants with the most preference points are drawn frst. However, Montana
law does state that 25% of nonresident combo licenses will go to people with zero preference points.

preference points are reset to completely at random, separately from those with preference points.
zero.
MCA 87-2-115: establishes that 75% of nonresident combination licenses shall be awarded in order of which applicants have purchased the great-
est number of preference points, and the other 25% are awarded to applicants who have not purchased any preference points.

Be Bear
Aware!
Black bear hunters must be
able to tell the difference
between a grizzly and a black
bear because grizzly bears
cannot be legally hunted in
Montana.
• Learn how to identify bears.
• Be absolutely sure of your
target.
If in doubt,
don’t shoot.
Carry Bear Spray and Know How To Use It!
– Hunting puts you at risk of encountering a bear. Calling game and using
scents may attract bears. General Bear Distribution in Montana
– If you hunt alone, let someone know about your plans. If you feel uneasy
hunting alone, hunt with a partner.

2026-2027 Sunrise-Sunset Tables For Determining Hunting Hours
These tables, including adjustments for daylight savings time, are the ofcial sunrise-sunset tables adopted by the Montana Fish &
Wildlife Commission for hunting in Montana. Authorized hunting hours for the taking of antelope, deer and/or elk begin one-half hour
before sunrise and end one-half hour after sunset each day of the hunting season. (Do not use other sources).
